5 Tips To Make Sure You Have A Fun Time With Castlevania: Belmont’s Curse

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The great thing about Castlevania: Belmont’s Curse is that it’s a rat’s nest of puzzles, hidden rooms, and platforming challenges that’s actually enjoyable to sit down and untangle. There’s no puzzle or secret so esoteric as to be utterly confounding, but nor is it too simple. Pay enough attention, and you’ll never need a guide to get you through. However! We all could use a little help now and again to traverse the game’s rougher edges. I’ve got five tips for you that’ll make your time with Belmont’s Curse that much more enjoyable.

Through gameplay settings, developer Evil Empire has built into the game the ability for anyone, regardless of skill level, to enjoy it. And while I understand that for a very specific kind of gamer, the use of such settings is anathema, they really can be a boon. You don’t have to simply turn off deathâwhich you can doâyou can also tweak how much damage Rose takes or deals out. Increasing Rose’s experience gains can make her get stronger, faster if combat is an issue. You can choose the option to respawn outside of a boss door to cut down on runback fatigue. If traps like spike pits are messing you up as you try to tackle the game’s tricky platforming challenges, you can adjust how much damage you take from them. Even adjusting your standard allotment of healing potions can be enough to offset any difficulties you might have without bruising the ego too much.

There is so much communicated with your map beyond simply where you are in the world. Paying attention to a room’s color can let you know if you’re missing a secretâa brown background represents a completed room, a purple one still has something not yet discovered. In places like Dracula’s Castle and Notre Dame, the map will render important rooms with specific detail and can provide puzzle hints if you pay close attention. For example, it’ll tell you how many chains you need to break for the Sage and the general direction of where to find them. Use those map markers, too. You get 50 of them to place anywhere you want to make it easy to backtrack or mark things you haven’t discovered a use for yet.

Belmont’s Curse is stuffed with unique weapons, and among the seven types of armaments is bound to be one that’ll suit your play style. But while some of the weapons you find pack a decent punch, Rose’s tarot cards are where your true offensive and defensive capabilities are unlocked. Each boss you defeat grants you a new spell and, if you complete the little side-quests called Works of Mercy associated with each one, you can make them stronger. Upgrading your spells via Works of Mercy is akin to unlocking easy mode. The spells are so damn powerful that, when combined with a high-enough level for Rose, you can make many boss fights trivial. My favorite is the spell earned from the Brittle King. Fully upgrading it not only makes Rose nigh unkillable, it also adds a bit of extra damage oomph that has saved my ass in combat arenas more than once. And! A lot of the Works are simple enough to complete. Killing 25 haunted armor enemies is one Work of Mercy you can complete for the Brittle King, and you’ve already done that several times over in the time it takes to get to him.

I’ve already mentioned this elsewhere, but the mirrors are more than just save points and the game isn’t the greatest about telling you that. This is another one of those “pay close attention for a neat reward” things, but kneeling in front of one (after unlocking Dracula’s Castle) will unlock fast travel. Kneeling teleports you to a hidden area home to (almost) all of the mirrors you’ve unlocked in an area. Pick where you want to go and the mirror will get you there. The mirror world itself is a puzzle to be solved so I won’t spoil more, but until you’ve solved it, fast travel only works within a zone. You’ll have to rely on the Ferryman to travel between them.

This one is highly specific, but it is helpful for the middle of the game. After unlocking Dracula’s Castle, the next area you’ll delve into is Notre Dame. There, the final boss room is blocked off by three different lasers that you shut down one by one. There are also tons of secrets and challenges that make use of the lasers to increase their difficulty. Instead of trying to juggle laser beams sniping at you as you go for a chest hidden at the end of a dangerous room, focus on defeating the boss as soon as possible, then go back and try the challenges you missed in the old church. Turning off the lasers guarding the boss door turns them off everywhere else, making a lot of Notre Dame’s platforming challenges much easier.

President Trump issued a declaration yesterday (Nov. 3), proclaiming space open to all humanity for exploration, and the right of American citizens to develop and settle the solar system according to United States laws.

"The American Declaration of Freedom in Space," describes space as the "province of all humans" and asserts that the United States will lead humanity's expansion into the cosmos. The proclamation reiterates the administration's goals with NASA's Artemis program to return American astronauts to the moon by 2028 and eventually sending astronauts to Mars, but introduces no new funding, programs or policy directives.

"Our Nation will lead the way in the exploration, development, use, and settlement of these new frontiers — forging an interplanetary civilization founded on the enduring and inalienable rights of freedom and individual liberty," the document says.

Trump invoked America's "frontier spirit," highlighting Apollo 11 astronauts as the first people in history to walk on the moon's surface and robotic rovers exploring Mars as examples of U.S. leadership, and emphasized the country's flourishing commercial space sector helping ensure "American space superiority for all eternity."

"The nation that leads in space will shape the future of humanity," the declaration says, and pledges that America will carry the values of faith, freedom and individual liberty into an interplanetary civilization.

The proclamation also highlights the success of NASA's Artemis II mission in April, which carried four astronauts on a 10-day journey around the moon and set a record for the farthest humans have ever traveled from Earth. The follow-up to that mission, Artemis III, is scheduled to launch next summer (2027), and will test rendezvous and docking operations between NASA's Orion spacecraft and the program's two commercial lunar landers in low Earth orbit.

Artemis IV will be the program's first lunar landing mission, and is targeted for 2028. A December 2025 executive order from the President reaffirmed the goal of meeting that timeline, as well as the deployment of the initial elements for a permanent lunar outpost by 2030.

Trump's declaration yesterday does not establish new NASA programs, allocate additional funding or issue instructions to federal agencies. Its sweeping language, though, does echo some of the principles in the 1967 Outer Space Treaty, which guarantees freedom of exploration while prohibiting national sovereignty claims on other planets.

He has worked the space beat since 2016, covering the evolution of NASA's commercial spaceflight partnerships and crewed missions, planetary science missions, the Artemis program and humanity's return to the moon. He also enjoys building 1:144-scale model rockets and spacecraft. Find some of Josh's launch photography on Instagram, and follow him on X, where he mostly posts in haiku.

Winter Storm Watch vs. Winter Storm Warning: What’s the Difference?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A winter storm watch and winter storm warning mean different things. Here's what each alert signals and how to prepare for dangerous weather.

Tracking severe weather can sometimes feel like it requires a degree in meteorology. With complex scientific terms and technical jargon, it can be difficult to understand what it all means and what should be done to prepare. That’s when the difference between a winter storm watch and a winter storm warning matters.

Both alerts come from the National Weather Service, but they call for different levels of urgency. Here’s how the alerts work and what homeowners should do when they see one.

A winter storm watch means a significant winter storm is possible, but forecasters aren’t yet sure where or when it will hit or how severe it will be. According to the National Weather Service, watches often arrive a day or two before a storm and can cover heavy snow, sleet, or ice.

They give residents time to prepare, even though the storm may not reach their area. As the forecast becomes clearer, the National Weather Service may upgrade the watch to a warning, issue an advisory, or cancel it altogether.

A winter storm warning means dangerous winter weather is expected or already happening. Heavy snow, sleet, or ice could make travel unsafe and cause other problems, including power outages. The amount of snow or ice needed to trigger a warning varies by location.

“Each office knows the local area and will issue Warnings, Watches or Advisories based on local criteria,” according to the National Weather Service. “For example, the amount of snow that triggers a ‘Winter Storm Warning’ in the Northern Plains is typically much higher than the amount needed to trigger a ‘Winter Storm Warning’ in the Southeast.”

Warnings generally arrive closer to a storm’s arrival than watches, although forecasters can issue one before snow or ice begins falling.

A winter weather advisory means snow, sleet, or ice is expected to cause problems, but forecasters don’t anticipate conditions severe enough for a warning. An advisory may signal slick roads or other travel disruptions. While the weather may be less severe than during a warning, residents should still take precautions and monitor local forecasts.

A winter storm watch gives homeowners time to prepare before conditions worsen. That includes checking heating systems, protecting exposed pipes from freezing, and stocking up on food, medications, and other essentials.

“Your primary concerns at home or work during a winter storm are loss of heat, power and telephone service and a shortage of supplies if storm conditions continue for more than a day,” according to the National Weather Service.

Homeowners should have flashlights, spare batteries, and a charged phone in case the power goes out. Checking smoke and carbon monoxide alarms, locating snow shovels, and stocking up on ice melt can also help.

Once a warning takes effect, homeowners should finish their preparations and avoid unnecessary travel. Staying indoors is generally safest during severe winter weather.

If the power goes out, residents can close off unused rooms and wear extra layers to stay warm. Portable generators should always remain outdoors, away from windows and doors. Grills and ovens should never serve as heating sources because they can create serious safety hazards.

Residents should continue monitoring weather alerts throughout the storm. Once conditions improve, homeowners can check for damage and begin clearing snow and ice from walkways and driveways.

National Weather Service, “Winter Weather Warnings, Watches and Advisories” National Weather Service, “Winter Weather Resources and Frequently Asked Questions” National Weather Service, “Prepare! Don’t Let a Winter Storm Take You by Surprise” Federal Emergency Management Agency, “Be Prepared for a Winter Storm”

Two Hikers Dead in Bear Attack in British Columbia, Leaving Officials to Look for Answers

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A young grizzly in British Columbia (Photo: Colleen Gara / Getty Images)

Two hikers are dead after a suspected bear attack on Indigenous lands in British Columbia, provincial wildlife officials say. The incident comes just a year after a bear encounter in the same area left four people seriously injured.

Rescuers discovered one body on the afternoon of Nov. 2 near the Snooka West Trail in Bella Coola, leading to a search for a second, missing victim.

That search concluded late Tuesday night when searchers found the second hiker dead. Officials with the B.C. Conservation Officer Service (BCCOS) believe both were attacked by a grizzly bear, and efforts are ongoing to locate and trap any bears in the area for assessment.

Authorities and family members have since identified the pair as 48-year-old Jennifer Balcarras and her daughter, 20-year-old Selah Reed.

“It is not yet clear whether more than one bear was involved,” BCCOS Investigator Jeff Tyre told Backpacker. “Any captured bear will be assessed. If it is determined to have been involved in the attack, it will be dispatched in accordance with provincial human-wildlife conflict response guidelines to protect public safety.”

Family members first reported Balcarras and Reed overdue on Sunday, Nov. 1. Police and SAR operators found the first human remains on Monday afternoon, and called BCCOS due to evidence that suggested an animal was involved.

The victims’ remains have since been returned to their families, and the Snooka West Trail remains closed.

In the wake of the deaths, tribal authorities of the Nuxalk Nation, the First Nations people who live in the Bella Coola valley, expressed their support. Samuel Schooner, elected Chief of the Nuxalk Nation, said in a statement that the tribe understands firsthand the trauma this incident will inflict on the victims’ family members and on the survivors of last year’s attack.

That incident occurred near Highway 20 on Nov. 20, 2025, when a grizzly attacked a local school group taking a lunch break. Adults were able to repel the animal with bear spray and a bear banger, but not before it severely injured four people, including three children. The four individuals were rushed to the hospital in varying conditions, and seven others required care at the scene.

No one died, and investigators continued to search for grizzlies in the area, eventually determining three bears could have been involved. Over the following two weeks, officers captured eight adult and juvenile grizzlies for forensic testing. All eight animals were eventually collared and relocated after they could not be forensically connected with the attack.

A total of 24 officers were involved in the response to that attack. Some reportedly drove 14 hours overnight to hand-deliver samples to the Alberta Wildlife Forensic Laboratory. BCCOS described both the response and the incident as extraordinary.

“In my 34 years of experience, I have not seen an attack like this with a large group of people,” Kevin Van Damme, with BCCOS, told Global News at the time. “So this is extremely rare and I think the public needs to understand that part.”

As 2025 field operations concluded, the Conservation Office reported decreasing bear sightings and pointed out that grizzlies in the area typically transition from hyperphagia to denning in or around December.

It’s unclear how long the current search will continue. A spokesperson for BCCOS said investigators were unavailable for comment due to heightened field activity.

“Our thoughts are with the family of the victims during this tragic time,” the agency said. “We are in contact with the family to offer any support we can as we continue to investigate this incident.”

Uranium cubes reveal why the German nuclear project failed

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

“Our calculations suggest Heisenberg significantly underestimated the materials required.”

During World War II, physicists on the Manhattan Project worried that the Nazi German nuclear program might achieve the first nuclear bomb before the US and its allies. That’s not what transpired, but historians have debated just how close the Germans came to achieving criticality in their last attempt at building a reactor: the B8 pile. Werner Heisenberg, who led that effort, claimed that they were close and had only needed a little more uranium. But according to a paper published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences Nexus, that was not the case: the project simply didn’t have sufficient uranium and heavy water to build a working bomb, given the design they were using.

This particular research started in 2013, when University of Maryland physicist Timothy Koeth—a co-author on the new paper—received the gift of a mysterious heavy metal cube from a friend. He instantly recognized it as one of the uranium cubes used by German scientists in their unsuccessful attempt to build a working nuclear reactor. Only 14 such cubes are known to exist today.

In 2019, we reported on Koeth’s quest to track down the cube’s origins, as well as several other similar cubes that had somehow found their way across the Atlantic. At the time, Koeth and his partner in the quest, then-graduate student Miriam “Mimi” Hiebert, had tracked down ten such cubes around the US. For instance, the Smithsonian Institute had a German uranium cube in storage. They tracked a third cube to Harvard University, where it was regularly passed around to students in introductory physics classes as a curiosity.

Underpinning the Manhattan Project in the US was the fear that German scientists under Adolf Hitler’s Nazi regime would beat the Allies to a nuclear bomb. The Germans had a two-year head start, but according to Koeth, “fierce competition over finite resources, bitter interpersonal rivalries, and ineffectual scientific management” resulted in significant delays in their progress toward achieving a sustained nuclear reaction. German nuclear scientists were separated into three isolated groups based in Berlin (B), Gottow (G), and Leipzig (L).

Heisenberg headed the Berlin group, and as the Allied forces advanced in the winter of 1944, Heisenberg moved his team to a cave under a castle in a small town called Haigerloch—now the site of the Atomkeller Museum. That’s where the group built the B8 reactor. It resembled an “ominous chandelier,” per Koeth, because it was composed of 664 uranium cubes strung together with aircraft cable and then submerged in a tank of heavy water shielded by graphite to prevent radiation exposure.

As the German scientists were racing against time, Manhattan Project lead Lieutenant General Leslie Groves kicked off a covert mission dubbed “Alsos,” with the express purpose of gathering information and materials related to Germany’s scientific research. When Allied forces closed in, Heisenberg took apart the B8 experiment and buried the uranium cubes in a field, ferreting away key documentation in a latrine. (Pity Samuel Goudsmit, the poor physicist who had to dig those out.) Heisenberg escaped by bicycle, carrying a few cubes in a backpack.

As Heisenberg reported, the German scientists’ final experiment failed because the amount of uranium in the cubes was insufficient to trigger a sustained nuclear reaction. But Heisenberg was confident that “a slight increase in its size would have been sufficient to start off the process of energy production.” A model described in a 2009 paper supported that, showing the group would have needed only 50 percent more uranium cubes to make the design work.

Koeth and Hiebert uncovered a box of declassified documents about German uranium in the National Archives and found about 400 other uranium cubes from a separate reactor experiment by the Gottow group. They concluded that the combined inventory would have been more than enough to achieve criticality in the B8 reactor, suggesting that Germany’s secretive, isolationist approach hampered their nuclear program because the two groups weren’t sharing information or resources.

This latest paper builds on that earlier work but reaches a different conclusion. Koeth, Hiebert, and their co-authors analyzed two of the surviving uranium cubes, along with combing through historical records, technical reports, correspondence, and similar archival sources to learn more about the uranium, heavy water, and graphite blocks used for the B8 reactor experiment.

Among their findings: The B8 reactor would have needed between 1,292 and 1,775 uranium cubes to achieve criticality, and twice the heavy water produced by the Norsk Hydro plant in Vemork, Norway—the only source at the time—over the course of the entire war. In short, “Our calculations suggest Heisenberg significantly underestimated the materials required,” the authors wrote.

The authors also cited prior work noting key differences in American and German graphite production. The former used petroleum coke. While some of this supply reached Germany, the British naval blockade in January 1940 deprived the Germans of that vital resource. The German nuclear physicists had to use metallurgical coke from the Ruhr, which had twice as many impurities as American coke. (The US scientists had similar issues until they located an unusually low-boron coke in Pennsylvania.) Thus, “no natural uranium, graphite-moderated pile could have gone critical” with the materials available to the German scientists at the time.

PNAS Nexus, 2026. DOI: 10.1093/pnasnexus/pgag282 (About DOIs).

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

57 Uncommon Old-Fashioned Girl Names Loaded With Charm

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Because you love vintage names but know way too many friends who have Avas or Olivias.

What's old is new again, and that's also true for baby girl names. Vintage names, like those from the 1920s, have been popular for a while. But since these types of names with nostalgic charm keep climbing the baby name charts, finding one that's not super popular can feel impossible. And, you know, you may not want your kid to be another Abigail in a room filled with 10 other Abigails. If that sounds like where you're at in your baby-naming journey, you'll need to dig a little deeper into the archives to find truly uncommon old-fashioned baby girl names.

Still, many new parents consider this search well worth the effort. Luckily, there's a slew of traditional names from way back when that are charming and quirky and lovely — and can easily rival the classics like Ava or Beatrice. For those genuinely looking for their baby's name to stand out, you might want to try something stunning and standalone like Elspeth or Mamie. If those tickle your fancy, keep reading for a compilation of names with an unusual quality that's elegant, edgy, and will surely stand the test of time.

Agatha is a very unique option for little girls these days, but it wasn’t always! It stayed in the top 1000 baby names from 1900 to 1945, and hasn’t been seen since. The name Agatha means “good” or “honorable,” which is such a powerful meaning for your little one.

Although it peaked in the nation’s top 100 names in 1880, Agnes fell off the charts sometime in the ‘70s. But the Greek name, which means “pure,” is poised for a comeback. The nickname Aggie, in particular,” has loads of retro charm.

From the 1880s until around the early 1930s, Alma held tight in the top 100 names for girls in the United States. It dropped pretty dramatically in the late ‘90s and early ‘00s and has settled just inside of the top 1000. A name with origins in many cultures, it means “nourishing” or “soul.”

This elegant name started strong in the 1800s, tapered off, shot to its peak in the late 1960s, and has gradually waned in popularity since. Of British and French origin, it means “noble strength.”

Latin for “venerable” or “majestic,” Augusta is grand in all the right ways. It has big great-aunt-who’s-traveled-the-world energy. Huge selling point: Gussie is an unbearably cute nickname.

Barbara is a classic in every sense of the word, and it definitely has an old-fashioned feel. The name Barbara means “foreign” or “strange,” and was most popular from the ‘20s through the ‘60s. You can use Barbie, Babs, or Barb as a cute nickname, too.

Bernie is the absolute cutest girl nickname, and the name Bernadette really sells it. This old-fashioned choice means “brave bear” or “strong,” and it was most popular in the 1920s.

An oldie but a goodie of a Greek name, Beryl means “light green semiprecious gemstone” and is perfect for the baby girl who’s the jewel of your eye.

A feminine name of Hebrew origin, Beulah means “bride.” That might make you think of how fast your daughter will grow up, but look at it like this — that’s a long way off, and it’s something to look forward to when it eventually arrives. Although this name was ranked #123 for girls in the U.S. in 1880, it’s dropped to the #6000-ish range today.

You’d think this name would have seen a bigger resurgence recently with so much love for The Golden Girls! But Blanche, a French name meaning “white” or “pure,” currently sits at #11,298 in popularity for U.S. girl names. For context, it was a top 100 name in the 1880s.

Never a wildly common name, Cicely hovered around the #5,000 mark through most of the early 1900s before dropping to its current ranking of #11,351 for girls. It means “blind of self-beauty.”

The feminine version of Claude, the name Claudette means “lame,” which isn’t all that great — but at least it’s French! It also hasn’t been popular since 1973, so you know she’ll be the only one in her preschool class.

Although Cordelia fell out of favor a little in the late ‘90s, it’s been slowly creeping back up the charts. And why not? This lovely name of Latin origin has the sweetest meaning: “heart.”

This lovely name means “steadfast,” which is exactly what you want your kid to be (except maybe at bedtime!). It feels a little prim, sure, but you could also soften it up by calling her Connie.

This beautiful name is an elaborate form of Cora. It means “little heart,” which perfectly describes your little one. It’s also forever tied to Coretta Scott King, the civil rights leader who carried her husband’s legacy forward for decades.

While the name has had some recent traction thanks to Selling Sunset’s Davina Potratz, it’s a fairly uncommon sweet name that means “beloved” — a sweet nod to your beloved bundle of joy.

From the Greek word delphís, this beautiful baby name means “dolphin” — and how cute is that? The name is also derived from Delphi, the home of an oracle in Greek mythology. It currently ranks #3643 for girl names in the U.S.

Oh goodness, imagine a sweet little baby named Dorothea! It’s just too much. This moniker means “gift of God” and has Greek origins. You can choose nicknames like Thea or Dottie, too, and hasn’t been on the popular chart since 1970.

It’s surprising that this name ranks all the way down at #15927 on the list because it’s really freaking cute! Meaning “sweet, candy,” Dulcie is related to Dulce — the name that inspired Cervantes’ Dulcinea, Don Quixote’s chosen lady.

Want your baby girl to stand strong in her voice? Consider this name, which means “well-spoken” (and happens to be the name of the tough and tenacious Effie White from Dreamgirls). Although Effie ranked #63 in the 1880s, it sits at #2120 today.

I love the simplicity of Ellen. It’s so sweet and lovely, and conjures up images of girls being badasses while also being little dollops of joy — sugar and spice and everything nice. Ellen means “torch” or “bright light” and was in the top 100 of baby girl names for literal decades. Now, it sits at #920.

A strong and sturdy name of Hebrew and Scottish origin, Elspeth means “chosen by God.” While it hasn’t made much headway outside of the U.K., it has a childish charm that might be fit for a sweet comeback.

Is Ethel not the absolute sweetest? This name just brings such joy, and its meaning is “noble.” The last time it hit the popularity charts was in the ‘70s, so I think it’s definitely time to bring back this old-fashioned cutie.

This unique name of Greek origin is perfect for your little one as it means “generous gift.” It also stems from Greek mythology, giving off some goddess vibes, too.

Like Effie, this Greek name means “well-spoken,” but it also means “sweetly speaking” — and, well, that just sounds lovely. Eulalia has a soft, earthy appeal, too, as it’s the name of a tall green grass that grows in Eastern Asia.

Faye had a good run of popularity in the ‘30s, but otherwise hasn’t come close to that same acclaim. It’s still unique now, but I suspect that the fantasy book culture has brought Faye — which means “fairy” or “belief” — back into the fold.

Don't underestimate this name that you might associate with your great-aunt! Meaning "strength of a spear," the German moniker feels a bit flinty but has a cute, much softer nickname in Gertie.

This Welsh name means “land nation,” but it has a quaint and sweet sound to it that defies its definition. And of course, there’s the Motown legend Gladys Knight, which makes it even more legendary.

Henri, Etta, Rett — so many sweet nicknames can come from the name Henrietta. It hasn’t been on the popular lists since 1968, and Henrietta also means “home ruler.”

A form of Esther, Hester means “star.” And whatever, no one cares about the Hester Prynne baggage. In fact, that kind of makes this name even cooler. A heroine who refused to be shamed into submission? Heck yeah.

An abbreviated form of Katherine, this sweet name means "pure." Naturally, it's a purr-fect name for cat lovers. It's also been the name of many famous characters, from Kitty Bennet of Pride and Prejudice to Kitty Pryde of X-Men.

In Roman mythology, Lavinia was the wife of Aeneas and the namesake of an ancient city, so this comes with a bit of historical pedigree. It’s lush and lyrical, not fussy at all, and the nickname Vinnie is so unexpectedly perfect for a little girl.

This lilting name of Hebrew and Arabic origin has beautiful (and sort of mysterious) meanings of “dark beauty” and “born at night.” If you grew up obsessed with Anna Sewell’s classic novel Black Beauty, this could be the old-fashioned girl’s name for your little one.

If you love Haunted Mansion, you undoubtedly know Madame Leota. So, while this name is pretty uncommon (#14,360 for girls’ names in the U.S.), it’s beloved in the Disney fan community. Germanic, it means “of the people.”

Loretta means “laurel,” which has such a sweet, country vibe. Retta, Lo, Etta, Rettie — there are so many cute nickname options for this old-fashioned choice. In the 1910s and ‘20s, this name was always in the top 150, and then ended up in the top 100 for pretty much all of the ‘30s.

A lovely name that means “little and womanly,” Lottie is a diminutive of the French name Charlotte. It has a “je ne sais quoi” flair that is due for a comeback... especially with the popular characters of the same name from Disney’s The Princess and the Frog and Showtime’s Yellowjackets.

I just love this one so much — it sounds like a storybook character! The name Louise means “renowned warrior,” which is pretty amazing, and while it’s gaining some traction on the popularity charts, it’s still a unique choice. (It was most popular in the 1910s.)

How sweet is Mabel? The name literally means “lovable,” and while it has gotten more popular in recent years, it’s still a unique choice for your little one. Before 2013, it hadn’t been on the popular charts since 1964.

Thanks to Hilary Duff (or Parenthood fave Mae Whitman), this darling little moniker could make a comeback, but Mae is definitely still considered old-fashioned. It’s often a diminutive of names like Margaret or Mary, but it stands on its own very well. It’s just so spunky and cute. Mae also means “maiden” or “young woman” and was in the top 100 baby girl names from 1900 to 1920.

Maude is just so punchy, I’m obsessed. This cute old-fashioned girl name hasn’t been on the popularity charts since 1950, and it means “powerful in battle.” Hell yeah, girl!

This adorable name, a shorter version of Margaret or another form of Mary, was famously chosen by Meryl Streep for her daughter Mamie Gummer. It means “pearl” and “star of the sea,” both beautiful metaphors for your new treasure.

Midge stems from the Greek word for “pearl” — but you may better recognize it as the name of the title character in Prime Video’s hit series The Marvelous Mrs. Maisel. Although the name dropped off in popularity in the ‘70s, it’s making a gradual comeback, currently ranking #2603.

This elegant English name means “gentle strength” and encapsulates both maturity and tenderness, making it a fantastic choice for the well-rounded daughter you want to raise.

Millicent just sounds vintage right off the bat, and its popularity journey truly makes that case — it hasn’t been on the lists since 1965. You can go with Millie for a cute nickname, but I truly do think Millicent just sounds so cool. It also means “strong in work.”

Who can forget Moaning Myrtle from Harry Potter? But this cute name has a richer history than that. Its origins come from the “evergreen shrub,” which was sacred to the goddess Venus as a symbol of love. So, in some ways, this name could symbolize your everlasting love for your baby girl.

OK, this might be what you call your grandmother, but it has a cute ring to it, too. The Hebrew name, which means "grace," has a uniquely modern, feminine vibe that stands out.

This gender-neutral name of Old Norse origins boasts many meanings: “wealth,” “honor,” “respect,” “man’s defender,” and “ancestor’s relic.” So, if you’re pretty sure your kid is destined to be a wild card, Ola it is! This name ranked in the top 200s during the 1880s, but today it has settled in around the #6800 mark.

Patricia feels incredibly traditional, but Patty for a little girl? Come on, we’ve got to bring it back. Patricia means “noble” and is a variant of Patrick if you like the Irish connection.

Doesn’t this just sound like it belongs to the main character of a cozy British mystery? Greek for “beloved,” it boasts a bunch of punchy nicknames like Mena, Pippa, and Pip.

OK, OK, bear with me — Prudence is extremely old-fashioned, but it also has the delightful nickname of Prue! Prudence means “wise,” “skilled,” or “intelligent,” and was extremely common during the Middle Ages. (It hasn’t been in the top 1000 baby girl names since 1948, either.)

Thanks to Dolly Parton’s immense popularity, the name Jolene has shot up the rankings in recent years. But this twangy name, which is a variant of Rae, is a much less common option (#10,919 for girl names in the U.S.) that offers the same spirit.

Calling all Ravenclaws! Harry Potter fans who call this house home will surely recognize Rowena as the first name of its founder. Outside of its HP claim to fame, the name means “famous friend” and “pure spear.” Plus, it comes with the cool nickname Ro.

Oh, Susannah! Don’t you cry for me! Whether you spell it Suzanna, Susanna, or with the traditional H at the end, this name is a classic. Susannah means “lily,” so it has a sweet, nature-related connection and a ton of cute nicknames, from Susie (or Suzie) to Suze and Anna. It was only in the top 1000 of baby girl names from 1967 to 1978, and even then, it was still one of the more unique options.

Thelma was popularized by the 1887 novel by Marie Corelli, and it means “will” in Greek. It’s peak grandma chic! And if you have two daughters, Thelma and Louise is right there. (Kidding... mostly.)

Theodora is the feminine form of Theodore, meaning “gift of God.” It’s just so cute, and Theo is a darling name for a little girl (so is Teddy, honestly!). From 1954 to 2019, it didn’t even top the charts, but it’s making a slow comeback.

Wilhelmina is just so gorgeous, and I love all of the nicknames you can pick for this one. The name also means “resolute protector,” so it feels like a strong moniker for your little gal. Bonus: It hasn’t been on the popularity charts since 1952, so you know it’s unique.

This lovely Welsh name means "blessed peace," which, sure, doesn't exactly align with everyone's favorite Winifred — the iconic red-haired witch from Hocus Pocus. But the name has a sophisticated feeling to it, and the nickname Winnie is just so delightful.

Almost no one uses AI agents to book their Uber, company says

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Very few people are using personal AI agents to order Ubers, apparently. On the transportation giant’s third-quarter earnings call on Wednesday, the company said that only four rides were ordered through an AI agent during its busiest week in October: the week of Halloween.

While more people used an AI agent to order food through its delivery service, Uber Eats, the volume was still fairly small compared to the overall number of trips.

Uber’s chief financial officer, Balaji Krishnamurthy, told Wall Street analysts on the call that the company handled around 320 million trips and orders across Uber and Uber Eats during the week of Halloween, but only 500 Uber Eats orders were placed by an AI agent.

“It is very, very early and very limited traction that we are seeing so far,” said CEO Dara Khosrowshahi, referring to the low number of orders placed by AI agents. “Listen, we hope that there will be more traction.”

The surprisingly low numbers of AI-initiated transactions come amid growing hype for a new wave of AI agents that don’t just answer questions but also handle tasks on users’ behalf. There’s been an explosion of personal AI startups, including one of the buzziest, Instinct, which was founded last year and raised its most recent round of funding at a $10 billion valuation. Meta’s Muse also became the No. 1 app in Apple’s U.S. App Store last month, topping 10 million users, per Meta’s own reporting. The company said many were using it to shop and transact with businesses.

OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and SpaceXAI (Grok) are also now offering their own AI agents, as are numerous smaller startups.

Still, early numbers from public companies indicate the agentic web still has a long way to go. DoorDash, for instance, said last week on its earnings call that it has only received a few hundred orders from AI agents so far, as most customers are still placing orders through the DoorDash app.

Uber is seeing the same behavior, it seems. Khosrowshahi pointed out that the average time to book a ride is only 50 seconds in the company’s app, and re-ordering a favorite food only takes 90 seconds. That suggests an AI agent isn’t necessarily saving anyone time.

Despite the low number of AI-driven transactions, Khosrowshahi seemed bullish on the idea of agents in general. “We think that is another opportunity to reach our platform. We think it will be a net positive in terms of demand on the ecosystem,” he said.

Right now, there are around eight major AI assistants on the market, Khosrowshahi noted, and he expects that number to grow to more than 10 in time.

“Every single AI system company is talking to us,” he added.

An Expert Guide to Nail Health, According to Professionals

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Here’s exactly how to keep them strong and healthy

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As manicure trends evolve with new lacquers, changing shapes, and viral techniques, beauty lovers often compromise nail health for aesthetics. While many nail formulas have improved over the years to prioritize strength, uniformity, and finish, experts say incremental habits—from lifestyle shifts to resisting the urge to peel off chipped polish—can ultimately play the biggest role in keeping your nails in excellent condition.

“The future of manicures isn’t just about the latest color trends or how long your gel polish lasts, but how well we care for the nails underneath,” Krisztina van der Boom, founder of DryBy, previously told Harper’s Bazaar. Hand, foot, and nail expert Margaret Dabbs agrees, urging nail enthusiasts to “Adopt a dedicated nail care routine and stick to it.” But what does an effective routine actually look like? Along with keeping your nails clean and hydrated with an emollient moisturizer, like Caudalíe’s Vinotherapist Hand and Nail Cream, professionals suggest keeping a cuticle oil, such as Dior’s Huile Abricot, in your bag for quick touch-ups.

“Cuticle oil is the one product I would never skip,” says Peach Bling founder Sandra Wei. “Look for a formula with jojoba oil, which has small enough molecules to absorb into the nail and surrounding skin.” Wei encourages her clients to apply it daily, ideally before bed, and to treat it as a small ritual. “A rich hand cream and gloves for dishwashing and cleaning also make a real difference,” continues Wei, “since water and harsh detergents are among the most common causes of dry, weak nails.” Maintaining nail and cuticle hydration is also key for mature nails, as they tend to grow more slowly and hold less moisture as we age, which can contribute to brittleness and vertical ridges.

Professionals have debated whether it’s safe to trim your cuticles, since cuticles guard nail beds against bacteria and infection; that's why they often advise pushing them back instead of removing them entirely. “As long as you're not cutting any live skin and damaging your cuticles, gently pushing them back and buffing away excess skin will actually help improve the health and the appearance of your cuticles and will help make your manicure last as long as possible while looking salon-quality,” says Olivia Van Iderstine, vice president of education and manicure trend expert at Olive & June. Prep your fingertips with cuticle oil or a soak in warm, soapy water before a manicure, then use a disposable orangewood stick or stainless steel pusher, such as JinSoon’s HyperCare Cuticle Pusher + Reducer, to improve polish cohesion and create an elongated nail look.

Consistent, gentle filing can also keep nails strong, regardless of your shape preference, because a smooth surface guards against unwanted splits, rips, and tears that can cause long-term damage. While there’s nothing wrong with a budget-friendly emery board, professionals say investing in the right tools provides the right resistance without compromising your nails' integrity. Dabbs is a fan of crystal nail files because they can help prevent micro-splits that lead to peeling and breakage, though the best results depend on not overfiling.

And if you usually opt for gel polish manicures, they don’t have to be a deal breaker for nail health. Several options, from builder gel to Japanese gel and more, provide longevity, durability, and style without weakening natural nails.

“Japanese gel systems are known for their precision and for their focus on preserving the natural nail,” continues Wei. “The prep is minimal and gentle, so we avoid heavy buffing of the nail plate. The gel itself is applied in fine, structured layers that are flexible and sit close to the natural nail.” Peach Bling prioritizes formulas that are free of HEMA and TPO, two ingredients commonly linked to gel allergies and sensitivities. “The result is a manicure that looks refined, wears beautifully, and treats the nail underneath with care,” explains the founder. Bazaar editors love builder gel, as it works like Japanese gel, supporting natural nails with a rounded apex to reinforce their sturdiness.

Experts advise keeping a gel set on for no longer than four weeks, as regrowth changes the nail's balance, ultimately causing unwanted stress and making breaks more likely. Once gel polish begins to lift or crack, it’s often a sign that removal is necessary—and that process can often be more damaging than simply wearing a gel formula in the first place. “What protects nail integrity is how the gel is applied and, even more importantly, how it is removed,” adds Wei. “With careful, gentle removal, there is no need to take a ‘break’ between appointments.”

We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, hair care, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

Five Pieces of Jewelry Calder Made for His Family Head to Bonhams With $300 K. Estimate

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Alex Calder is perhaps best known for inventing the mobile—a kinetic sculpture in which abstract shapes are gently moved by natural air currents—but the late, great American sculptor was also a talented jeweler.

In fact, Calder, known to many as “Sandy,” produced approximately 1,800 unique, handcrafted pieces of wearable art throughout his life, according to the Met, which held the first museum exhibition dedicated to his jewelry in 2008. He famously created custom jewelry for his closest family and friends—five pieces of which will go under the gavel at Bonhams this fall. Related Articles In Postwar Rural France, Alexander Calder Found Respite from Pressures of Fame and a Chance to Dream Big How Collectors Jon and Kim Shirley Stepped In to Restore the Senate Building's Famed Calder

Calder made jewelry as bold and dynamic as his sculpture, constantly experimenting with materials and forms. His avant-garde creations, often in brass, copper, and silver, became popular with 20th-century high society, artists, and celebrities, including socialite Peggy Guggenheim, painter Georgia O’Keeffe, and actress Anjelica Huston, to name but a few.

Calder’s jewelry continues to resonate with high-profile names, too: Just this past September, reality TV star-turned-model Kendall Jenner wore a pair of his hand-hammered silver earrings at the Venice Film Festival. (They originally belonged to the wife of renowned graphic designer Ivan Chermayeff, according to Louisa Guinness, the London-based gallerist who gave them to Jenner.)

The five pieces heading to auction were each made as a gift for a woman in Calder’s family: his sister, his mother, and his niece. They are entirely bespoke, made for that particular person. Because Calder refused every offer to mass-produce his jewelry, any piece that comes to market is a rare find. This quintet has remained in private hands for nearly 70 years, making it rarer still.

The auction star is a necklace Calder made for his sister Margaret Calder Hayes. “Peggy” was a close confidante, a lifelong muse, and a recipient of his early works. Though it was executed circa 1940, the silver piece showcases hand-wrought curves that feel decidedly modern. It is expected to hammer down for between $100,000 and $150,000.

Also on offer are sculptural silver earrings (estimate: $40,000–$60,000) that Calder gifted Peggy, which look very similar to the pair Jenner wore at Venice. Rounding out the five are two silver brooches spelling out the initials of Calder’s mother (Nanette) and niece (Eva), and a spiral-shaped silver ring for his niece.

Bonhams has valued the collection at nearly $300,000. It will be on view at the auction house’s new N.Y.C. flagship from November 7, before crossing the block in the Post-War & Contemporary Art sale on November 19.

Democratic sweep of Congress forces crypto industry to rethink Washington strategy

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

As results rolled in, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate.

After two years of a pro-crypto, pro-innovation climate in Washington, the digital asset industry faces a sharp political jolt as Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress, forcing the sector to seek new allies within a party that has been more divided on crypto.

As results rolled in on Tuesday, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate. Eight anti-crypto Senators were also elected alongside 106 in the House.

Following the results, some in the crypto industry have taken the position that crypto is bipartisan and that lawmakers on both sides of the aisle are pro-digital assets. But a Democratic sweep means more oversight, and there is close to no chance of the crypto industry getting legislation it wants on the table.

"Nobody has their head in the sand," said Kara Calvert, vice president of U.S. policy at Coinbase, in a post on X. "There will be headwinds for any bipartisan legislation next year, but crypto is 100% bipartisan."

Many of the new members in Congress are pro-crypto, said Kevin Wysocki, head of policy at Anchorage Digital.

"The younger members are more used to this technology, are more open to new technology that maybe rivals some of the existing technology," he said in an interview with The Block on Wednesday. "It's very bullish, I think, for crypto overall, so I think that's one of the great things from what happened yesterday, and I think it's only going to get better over time, regardless of who's in power, Republicans or Democrats."

However, one of the biggest races the crypto industry had its eyes on, and money on, was the Ohio Senate race between former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown and SWC-endorsed Republican Sen. Jon Husted. The crypto political action committee spent tens of millions of dollars on that race against Brown, but ultimately lost when Brown defeated Husted later in the night.

Brown was previously Senate Banking Committee Chair, which has jurisdiction over digital assets and has been viewed as critical of crypto. He could have a leading role in oversight and regulation of the industry. And since he won in a special election to fill Vice President JD Vance's Senate term, that means Brown could return to the Senate as early as Dec. 15.

Brown's return is significant, Wysocki said. When he was chair of the Senate Banking Committee, Brown and his Senate staff were open to constructive conversations about crypto, he said.

Pro-crypto political action committees reportedly spent over $60 million alone in the Senate race in Ohio against Brown. On its own, crypto PAC Fairshake raised $106.2 million, according to Americans for Financial Reform. And crypto PACs overall spent $170 million on the midterms, according to Fortune, with millions going to Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts in Nebraska.

Fairshake spent $154.5 million in both primaries and the general election, with over $13.5 million going to Ricketts.

"Fairshake played a meaningful role in winning competitive races across the country in 2026 and we will do it again in 2028," said Geoff Vetter, spokesperson for Fairshake, in an emailed statement to The Block. "Newly elected and current members will have a choice: protect consumers and American jobs or fight progress. We will be watching and '28 is a new map."

One crypto industry source alluded that PACs and trade associations will have to "recalibrate" and need to "double down on being bipartisan."

Specifically on Fairshake, the person criticized funds being used on Brown's race.

"What did we ultimately get out of that?" they said. "Where could that money have been better spent? To be perfectly honest with you, I think it could have been better spent on shoe-leather lobbying."

That money could have been spent to lobby lawmakers on the Clarity Act, broad crypto legislation that passed the House, but got stuck in the Senate in September, they said.

"You spend a fraction of it, and you could have effectively hired all of DC to lobby on Clarity," they added.

Focus will turn to taxes and security and hacks, Anchorage's Wysocki said. The election, too, comes just days after the largest crypto hack on record of Binance for over $6 billion.

"Congress is going to be asking those questions, like how do we make security at our financial institutions stronger?" Wysocki said.

The Clarity Act that passed out of the House last year and stalled in the Senate a few months ago is unlikely to advance over the next two years given Brown's win, said investment bank TD Cowen.

Oversight will also come into view, including investigations into Trump-family-run World Liberty Financial, said TD Cowen’s Washington Research Group, led by managing director Jaret Seiberg.

"Democrats will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements, including World Liberty Financial," Seiberg wrote in a note on Wednesday. "We believe the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Hong Kong artists create a poetic moment in Venice

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Kingsley Ng’s installation Laundry Nocturne (2026) Photo © Hong Kong Museum of Art

For Fermata: Hong Kong in Venice, the Hong Kong-born artists Kingsley Ng and Angel Hui are training visitors’ attention on the quiet moments and subtle details connecting both cities. The exhibition, marking the 25th anniversary of Hong Kong’s first participation in the Venice Biennale, is curated by the Hong Kong Museum of Art (HKMoA) and continues until 22 November. It is housed in a historic Venetian residential building in the floating city’s Campo della Tana neighbourhood, a setting that underlines the meditative, lived-in quality to the works.

“What we hope visitors take away from the exhibition is a moment of reflection,” says Dr Maria Mok, the director of the HKMoA. “Through Kingsley and Angel’s attention to everyday details, we hope visitors feel invited to slow down and become more attentive to their surroundings, even within the hustle and intensity of urban life.”

One of Kingsley Ng’s installations, for instance, Laundry Nocturne (2026), consists of a projection and soundscape that evoke the play of light across laundry hung between buildings. It was inspired by the artist’s experience visiting Venice, where billowing laundry on clotheslines is still common, though for Ng it also evoked memories of Hong Kong.

Kingsley Ng in Venice Photo © Hong Kong Museum of Art

“The laundry becomes a point of entry into a wider soundscape of nighttime labour: recordings from a wastewater treatment plant, a newspaper-printing facility and a postal sorting centre evoke the work that continues while much of the city sleeps,” Ng says. “These modest, often overlooked processes that keep a city alive do not only connect Venice and Hong Kong. They are like a low hum in almost every city, to be picked up by an ear that doesn’t only listen to loud grandeur.”

For her installations, Angel Hui shines a spotlight on humble features of Hong Kong’s everyday life, including goldfish in plastic bags and a neighbour’s ornate but faded window frame. In her magical rendering, glowing plastic bags embroidered with goldfish motifs are suspended overhead, while a handwrought iron window made by Hui and traditional metalsmiths rotates slowly in the gallery.

“In Hong Kong, a goldfish bag is an everyday object; in Venice, it seemed almost precious—delicate and exposed,” Hui says. “The experience reminded me that a work does not need to be grand to hold meaning. Sometimes, its poetry lies in inviting us to look more attentively, and to meet the familiar with tenderness.”

Angel Hui’s installation I Would Like to Open a Window for You (2026) Photo © Hong Kong Museum of Art

Both artists, through their site-specific installations, encourage visitors to mark a pause while heightening their attention to details of light, sound, colour and texture. This invitation to contemplation is echoed in the exhibition’s title, Fermata, which is a type of musical symbol instructing a performer to hold a note or a pause for an extra moment, and the term for the places where Venetian vaporettos stop to let off and pick up passengers. For Hui, this title and its dual meanings resonate across the exhibition’s disparate locales.

“The difference in pace is pronounced: Hong Kong often presses forward, while Venice seems to make room for pause,” she says. “This is why Fermata—a moment of stopping, of stillness—felt so resonant. In both cities, the ordinary endures, and in that endurance, everyday objects acquire a quiet radiance.”

The exhibition’s title and installations also complement the theme of this year’s Venice Biennale, In Minor Keys, set by the late curator Koyo Kouoh.

Angel Hui in Venice Photo © Hong Kong Museum of Art

“I come to see the works as experiences to return to and live alongside,” Ng says. “For me, that is one meaning of In Minor Keys: making time for the ordinary and immediate—the air, light and weather around us—and becoming more attentive to our presence within them.”

The artists’ ability to turn familiar objects and moments into tableaux where time pauses and senses are heightened is key to the exhibition’s power. It allows their installations to resonate across localities, from the Venetian Lagoon to Hong Kong’s cityscape.

“The artworks merge into the Venetian neighbourhood, from laundry hanging across nearby alleys and above our courtyard exhibition space to the view of the neighbourhood outside Kingsley’s Laundry Nocturne gallery, to wrought-iron windows that echo Angel’s work,” Dr Mok says. “All these details are blurring the boundary between the exhibition and its surroundings. They also show how small moments of daily life can quietly synchronise across different cities and audiences.”

From a Christie's exhibition to a posthumous display of Mel Ramos, this year numerous explicitly commercial shows signal a shift in attitude

Apple turns iCloud into spy cloud, will start looking at public share links

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Content shared with anyone who has the URL will be scanned for 'harmful content,' though the iMaker hasn't defined that term

If you share content using public iCloud links, Apple could soon be looking over your shoulder and checking for "harmful content." The iBiz announced plans to moderate publicly accessible iCloud share links in version 27.3 of iOS, iPadOS, macOS, and visionOS on a support page published on Tuesday. To clarify, this applies only to iCloud content shared via links created to be accessible to anyone, as opposed to content shared directly with other people.

“Items shared using Anyone with the Link will be checked by Apple for harmful content,” Apple explained on the new help page without bothering to define what harmful content means. Based on the wording, it appears those checks will apply to all iCloud items shared using "Anyone with the Link," which Apple says it can also access. The company hasn't explained how the checks will work.

The share-with-anyone option removes end-to-end encryption from the shared item, Apple said, even if Advanced Data Protection, Apple's optional end-to-end encryption feature for supported iCloud data, remains enabled for the account.

“If you try to upload harmful content, Apple reserves the right to move, refuse, modify, remove, or delete the content at any time,” the help page continues, adding that the iMaker may also block or disable accounts associated with harmful content.

The company also said it reserves the right to involve law enforcement when necessary, suggesting that its undefined "harmful content" category could include child sexual abuse material (CSAM), malware, and other potentially unlawful content.

Without a proper definition of what it defines as harmful, however, iCloud users are essentially left at Apple’s mercy: If it chooses to continue to bend the knee to the Trump administration, for example, content deemed objectionable by the standards of political figures could end up wiped out.

Apple also hasn't explained whether human moderators, automated systems, or both will be responsible for identifying and removing so-called harmful content, or whether any decisions will be left to AI, which can easily be fooled into making a bad moderation call.

The scanning of such content, as mentioned above, begins with version 27.3 of Apple’s various OSes, which were released to developers in beta on Tuesday, with a general release expected in December.

Apple didn’t respond to questions for this story. ®

This Cult Sci-Fi Horror Series With Stephen King Vibes Has Become A Major Netflix Hit

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

There are so many problems with streaming media, but a big one is that each of these companies is hellbent on creating new movies and shows to fill out their library of licensable titles. There's a lot that's worth watching, but there's nowhere near enough time to watch these worthy works. As a result, great films and series fall between the cracks or get unceremoniously cancelled before they can complete their run.

So it's something of a miracle that "From," a science fiction horror series created by John Griffin that flew under the radar for several years, survived for five seasons. The final season was greenlit by MGM+ last April, thanks in large part to the show's enthusiastic cult following, but the company might consider lobbying Griffin for another season. Because this cult favorite has become a bona fide hit on Netflix.

Netflix began streaming the first three seasons of "From" last month, and it's currently the fourth most popular series on the service. It's not hard to understand why. Griffin's show is basically "Lost" with major Stephen King vibes, and it even features one of the show's stars, Harold Perrineau.

"From" opens with a family of four being forced to re-route their road trip when their chosen path is blocked by a fallen tree. They wind up in a small town where they receive directions out of the area from Sheriff Boyd Stevens (Perrineau), but they soon find themselves back in the town again. Gradually, they discover that there is no escaping this unnamed town.

"From" quickly makes "Lost" look like a lovely tropical vacation when it's revealed that the town turns decidedly lethal at night, due to the presence of vicious, human-looking monsters. And the show is unsparing in depicting these creatures' savagery. It's a gorehound's delight.

A major reason why "From" never caught on with mainstream viewers is that the first season streamed on Epix, which would eventually be acquired by MGM+. So Netflix is essentially doing a huge favor for MGM+, because "From" is every bit as addictive as "Lost."

Given the series' uptick in visibility, perhaps the critically acclaimed show (all four seasons rate over 90% on Rotten Tomatoes) will finally earn some Primetime Emmy nominations. It won the Saturn Award for Best Horror Television Series last year, and Perrineau is well overdue for Emmy consideration. The only impediment here is that voters will have to binge four seasons of a show that was off their radar.

If voters do take the plunge, they'll find a smart, terrifying series that flirts with the bleakness of Stephen King's legendary horror bummer "The Mist." A lot of people may not have the stomach for that, but Griffin and his writers adroitly plumb the darkness of our era, where days often feel like a purgatorial drag. But "From" keeps us watching because we need to believe there's a way out of this.

Billy Joel at Home: 10 Photos of the Piano Man Through the Decades

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For Billy Joel, New York is more than just a place. It’s an inextricable part of what makes the singer who he is. “My speech patterns are New York. The music that I like, it was all New York–based. That’s what I think of when I think of home,” he said in his 2025 HBO documentary Billy Joel: And So It Goes. “It’s my roots.”

The Bronx native grew up in Hicksville, a middle-class suburb of Nassau County on Long Island. His family struggled to make ends meet. “There was poor, and then there was Billy Joel poor. He was lucky if he had enough money to buy a pack of cigarettes,” his childhood friend and fellow musician Jon Small said in And So It Goes. According to Joel’s sister Judy, no matter how hard times were, their mother Rosalind insisted he take piano lessons. His talent “had to be nurtured,” Judy said in the HBO doc. “She knew that he was going to be something someday.”

Before he indeed became something, Joel worked on an oyster boat. “I used to look up at this mansion on the hill and wonder what it would be like to live in a house like that,” he said in the documentary, referencing the waterfront property on Centre Island he nicknamed MiddleSea. “I used to think, ‘They’re rich bastards. They never had to work a day in their life.’ Well, I own that house now. It’s not finished yet. And neither am I.”

Read on for a glimpse into the Grammy winner’s life, from his early days in New York to his brief stint in Los Angeles and return home.

Joel’s music career took the Long Island native to Los Angeles in the early 1970s, and he signed with Columbia Records in 1973. After three years in LA, he and his first wife, Elizabeth Weber, returned to his home state, where he completed his fourth studio album, Turnstiles. “I wanted to get back to New York and make more of a rock and roll album,” Joel explained in And So It Goes. “I was on a bus on my way back to Highland Falls, New York. Elizabeth found us a house up on the Hudson River, and it was fall. It was just gorgeous. The colors were ablaze, the trees were, like, gold and red, and I remember thinking, ‘This is the New York State that I loved when I was a kid. This is magic. We don’t have this in California.’ And I wrote this love song to New York on the bus.” It would become “New York State of Mind,” one of Joel’s most popular tunes.

The singer-songwriter poses with some of his records in January 1978.

Joel’s fifth studio album, 1977’s The Stranger, was a runaway success—but the singer, pictured alongside his accolades in New York in 1978, told Playboy a few years later that his newfound fortune didn’t faze him. “I read these stories about Peter Frampton’s multimillion-dollar mansion, 15 Rolls-Royces and all that,” he said. “Who cares? It’s all bullshit. What was his last record like? Was it any good? That’s the question. People get hung up on the money thing. It’s nice to have four motorcycles. It’s fun. And I walk into the garage and go, ‘Holy shit! I’ve got four motorcycles!’ But I don’t go beyond a certain point.”

Joel, his then-wife Elizabeth, and their dog in 1978.

Instead of splurging on a million-dollar mansion, Joel told Playboy that when he and his wife decided to buy a home, “We got a $300,000 house with a mortgage like everybody else.” Their glass house, featuring floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the Long Island Sound, was located in the town of Oyster Bay on Long Island’s North Shore. Joel appears poised to hurl a rock at the home on the cover of his 1980 album, Glass Houses. “The old thing about people who live in glass houses shouldn’t throw stones—I don’t believe it,” he said. “I think, Why not? Take chances. I really wanted to throw a rock at my own house.”

Joel and Elizabeth at their Long Island residence.

In the 1970s, Joel asked Weber to be his manager. “The company I’m with now is New York–based,” Joel said of his representation. “It’s called Home Run—nothing to do with baseball. It’s a home-run agency.” Weber got right to work. She recalled in his HBO documentary that the “story [Joel] likes to tell” of her early days on the job is that he woke up the next morning to phones everywhere and “cabinets installed.” However, she said, “That’s not really true, but it’s close to true.” The couple (seen here at Joel’s grand piano in their living room) split time between their Manhattan apartment, where they worked, and Oyster Bay. Weber left her role managing Joel when the two split, and her brother Frank picked up where she left off. Home Run dissolved amid financial woes in the ’80s, and Joel eventually pressed charges against Frank over mishandled funds; the parties reached a settlement, the terms of which have not been disclosed publicly.

The musician mixing tapes in the music studio of his New York home.

The Oyster Bay house, a midcentury-modern residence, was not far from where Joel grew up in Hicksville. Photographed here in his at-home music studio in 1978, the singer’s career was on the upswing—but his relationship with Weber was growing tense. “Having a big boom in record success, I think my marriage was starting to unravel around then; 1979, it wasn’t the happiest time,” Joel once said. “Record success doesn’t translate into happiness.” Joel assumed things were paying off financially, but he admitted he “wasn’t aware” how much money he was actually bringing in. “I know we were living in a nice house, my wife was going out and buying all kinds of nice stuff,” he said. “I never really went out and did that.”

Joel multitasks in his LA home studio in 1984.

Though Joel had settled down on the East Coast, he told the Los Angeles Times that his California years cross his mind “all the time.” The singer, pictured here in LA in 1984, wrote his hit song “Piano Man” based on his experience as a lounge musician (using the name Bill Martin) at a now-defunct bar near the corner of Wilshire Boulevard and Western Avenue during the early 1970s. “I wonder what happened to the people I knew—the people in the bar in ‘Piano Man,’” he said. “It was the Executive Room in the Wilshire district. I got tips and made union scale, and I was able to afford an apartment in Studio City. The weather was great; the people were nice.”

Though it appears Joel lived like a rock star in Los Angeles—as seen here in 1984, perched on a sheepskin-style throw over a leather sofa with a pack of Marlboros and a landline telephone by his side—he told the Los Angeles Times in 2023 that he felt more like “an exiled writer living in Paris” during his early stint in the City of Angels. “I went from being an insignificant nobody to the Piano Man while I was there,” he said. “I still look back to my time in LA with a certain amount of romance.”

Joel is pictured here at the dinner table in 2001. When The New Yorker paid him a visit at his Centre Island residence in 2014, he described the interior’s aesthetic as “Catholic martyrs and saints, grotesqueries.” In the library, Joel—who decorated the place himself—had a model of the Queen Mary on his mantel, and in the living room facing the fireplace was a triptych of the Last Supper along with silver jugs and bowls on a nearby table.

Joel’s various properties have passed through some famous hands. He and his second wife, supermodel Christie Brinkley, owned a 12-acre oceanfront property in the East Hampton hamlet of Amagansett. The couple raised daughter Alexa Ray Joel in the Long Island dwelling. It was later sold to Jerry and Jessica Seinfeld for $32 million. According to The New Yorker, Joel also sold a duplex on Central Park West to singer-songwriter Sting for $5 million and a house on Martha’s Vineyard to businessman Mickey Drexler. At one point, Joel purchased a home in the Hamptons from Jaws star Roy Scheider.

“As a little kid, I always liked boats and the romance of living on an island,” Joel told Parade in 2018. The singer—pictured here on an East Hampton dock in 2001—has always felt at home on the water. In 2007 he designed a 57-foot powerboat called the Vendetta that can go about 50 knots, per The New Yorker. Boating represented “freedom,” Joel said in his documentary. “I am the captain of my ship.”

Cardiff City 3 – 3 West Ham | Final whistle, first impressions

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

IT took all of eleven minutes for the new manager bubble to burst as the home side opened the scoring and the Cardiff City fans duly launched into ‘you’re getting sacked in the morning’ to welcome West Ham’s Scott Parker into the dugout. To be fair, a cracking strike from Jack Moylan gave Hermansen no chance and as is now customary, the Hammers found themselves playing catch up.

Parker’s West Ham had set up with a back four holding a surprisingly high line, plus Magassa and Engels playing in front of the defenders but the pair were still bisected by the excellent pass that put Moylan through for his goal.

New manager, same misery as three went in before half time – again

Magassa and Kilman went into the book early on, the young Frenchman in particular looking short of match pace. Taty looked isolated – as we’d feared – and the Hammers looked a little short of ideas the half hour mark, hardly venturing into their opponent’s 18 yard box.

The warning signs were there as the awful Morato was beaten a couple of times as the Bluebirds attacked down the left: The stand in left back duly gave away a penalty, again out of position and clambering all over the back of his winger. Two-nil with Hermansen again beaten from a spot kick.

A third came from a corner, headed in at the near post by Moylan – a hat trick in the first half – and Scott Parker might have been questioning his rush to assume control ahead of the international break: Three nil before half time, three goals conceded for a third week in a row and the Hammers booed off by their own fans again.

Piroe warming up with Kanté and Mayers showed three changes at half time as Magassa, Morato and Taty departed.

Mukasa’s lobbed goal after being put through by Bowen put the Irons back in the game, with still half an hour to go and at least giving the travelling supporters something to cheer. Bowen, playing more centrally, started to pull the strings and Piroe went close. Suddenly, from nothing, a Bowen through pass was flicked on by Piroe and Engles, through on goal slotted home like a veteran striker. 3-2: And thousands of Hammers armchair fans were thinking maybe, just maybe.

This was, at least, a fighting response to a half time dressing down and a well deserved barracking from the away fans.

Kanté almost levelled down one end, Hermansen inexplicably flew out and clattered Kilman down the other – almost giving away a fourth in the process- and suddenly, you couldn’t take your eyes off it.

And then – three – three and the roof came off thanks to Max Kilman’s goal from Bowen’s brilliant cross.

Absolutely chaotic scenes in the away end as the incredulous claret and blue army couldn’t believe they’d witnessed a fightback from a team dead and buried at half time.

It could have been four as Trott stretched his fingers to Soucek’s header – agonisingly close as the minutes ticked by. Brilliant second half efforts by Piroe, by Bowen – the man of the match – and excellent impact from Ezra Mayers who might just have found himself a role in Parker’s side.

Nuno’s team in the first half, Parker’s in the  second.

Liverpool Have the Next Gerrard and Torres Who Can Save Andoni Iraola £200m

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Andoni Iraola could reap the benefits of an exciting duo that some Liverpool fans have compared to Steven Gerrard and Fernando Torres.

The Reds have enjoyed a relatively solid start to life under the Spanish head coach, who replaced Arne Slot in the summer. Having lost only twice in 14 games in all competitions, there are plenty of positives to take.

However, there are a few positions in the squad that either require major surgery or extra quality depth. Two of those are central midfield and up front. But that might not be the case for long.

Iraola showed he's more than willing to give youth a chance during his time at Bournemouth. The Spaniard has continued that trend at Anfield, handing big roles to teenagers Rio Ngumoha and Trey Nyoni in the opening months of the season.

One man who is certainly making a case to be the next in line for significant first-team minutes is Will Wright. The 18-year-old striker joined the Reds from Salford City for an initial £200,000 in 2025, and is now showing an incredible eye for the back of the net.

The Egyptian King has now played 10 games since his Liverpool exit, and plenty has been said about it.

On Wednesday afternoon, Wright fired in a hat-trick against Fenerbahce in the UEFA Youth League, having already notched a treble in the competition. His tally in all competitions for the youth side is seven goals and three assists in 12 appearances.

His third goal against Fenerbahce summed up the impressive Englishman, who latched onto a through ball and fired past the goalkeeper like an experienced striker. Watch it below.

Many Liverpool supporters have taken just as much interest in the player who threaded the ball through to Wright after a powerful run in midfield. Luca Eden, a 15-year-old midfielder, has already drawn comparisons to one of the club's greatest-ever players, Steven Gerrard.

Watch the clip above, some have suggested it took them back to the glory days of Gerrard and Torres, a partnership that excited fans and combined for many goals at Anfield.

Eden is already playing with players above his age group and looking comfortable, with his powerful strides and passing ability reminiscent of Gerrard.

It's extremely early days for the two youngsters, but if they progress to become the players fans expect, then Liverpool have two future stars in their ranks.

In 2026, the going rate for a top-class centre-forward is upwards of £100 million. A reliable goalscorer is worth their weight in gold, especially at the top level. Wright is showing early promise that he can become exactly that.

When Liverpool eventually look to replace Alexander Isak as the starting striker, Hugo Ekitike looks best placed to fill that void. By that time, Wright could be more than ready to play second fiddle to the Frenchman.

Found an error? Send it info@www.givemesport.com so it can be corrected.

Eden's chances of making it on the red side of Merseyside are harder to gauge given his age. However, midfielders like Enzo Fernandez (twice), Declan Rice and Elliot Anderson have all moved for £100 million or more. Living up to Steven Gerrard's name is almost impossible, but getting anywhere near that level would save Iraola and Liverpool from splashing out on another huge transfer fee.

17 better ways Elon Musk could have wasted $446 million

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →
