The Past, Present, and Future Exist All at Once, Some Physicists Say. Does It Mean Your Fate Is Fixed?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

This controversial idea about the universe could solve some of the biggest puzzles in quantum physics, like whether time travel could exist.

Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

What came first, the chicken or the egg? Normally we’d expect the explanation to run one way—the chicken laid the first egg, or the egg hatched the first chicken. But what if a future chicken could influence the egg before it hatched? That nonintuitive idea has weight when you consider a concept that some physicists are mulling over: that all of time exists all at once.

Welcome to the “block universe,” where the future influences the past, everything that will happen already has, and the nature of free will is debatable.

The way we perceive the cosmos normally is that time flows forward. We get older, our planet revolves around the sun, the universe moves inextricably forward from the Big Bang. But in the block universe interpretation of reality, we don’t actually need to invoke time in this way to explain how things work.

“There’s no flow of time in the block universe,” says Emily Adlam, PhD, an assistant professor of the philosophy of quantum mechanics at Chapman University. “Time is just another dimension, like all the dimensions of space.” It’s akin to the directions “left” and “right” we’re all familiar with, and which both exist simultaneously. Time’s directions—the past and the future—likewise both exist simultaneously, she explains.

Imagine looking into our solar system and first seeing Earth on one side of the sun, then on the other. Would that be proof that time has passed, or would it simply show that our planet’s orbit was carving a path through spacetime? The block universe argues the latter, and there’s a reason for that.

“Our understanding of the flow of time is flawed,” says Rod Sutherland, PhD, a theoretical physicist at the University of Sydney’s Centre for Time. “If you slowed time down to a half, or a quarter, or an eighth, it would be unobservable. It starts to throw doubt on what it means by the flow of time.”

The idea of a block universe dates back more than a century, but in recent years scientists have increasingly explored how a version of it might resolve some of the deepest puzzles of quantum physics.

In particular, Adlam and others have been studying a concept called retrocausality, where events in the future can influence events in the past. It turns out that this is a surprisingly good way to explain some of the peculiarities of the quantum world, which we still don’t understand. “There’s a number of paradoxical aspects of quantum mechanics that become a lot more understandable if you bring this in,” says Sutherland.

For example, in the quantum world two particles can be entangled and seem to share some properties across great distances of space. When they are both measured, they seem to have impossible correlations, implying that there must be some sort of “spooky action at a distance” between them, a term coined by Albert Einstein.

But how does this actually work? One possibility might be retrocausality, where the effect of something can precede the cause. “When you prove there must be spooky action at a distance, you assume the future can’t possibly affect the past,” says Ken Wharton, PhD, a professor of physics and astronomy at San José State University. “If the future could affect the hidden past, those theorems fail.”

Take an example where Alice and Bob are measuring two entangled particles, in two different locations. Under retrocausality, Alice can “cause some past variable, back where the particles were first entangled, and that variable would have also been sent to Bob,” says Wharton. “Some people call it zigzag causality. Then you don’t need spooky action at a distance. It’s just spooky retrocausal action.”

Wharton has also been theoretically exploring retrocausal models of quantum circuits (these are models for quantum computation where information flows along a timeline). That way, he can treat simpler cases as arbitrarily complicated systems. And it matters for us all, he says, in understanding exactly what our place in the universe is.

“It would be weird to have our choices affect the hidden past,” he says. “That would blow our minds. You get all of these weird consequences.” But it just so happens that these weird consequences “look a lot like quantum physics,” he says. “You could say that’s why quantum physics seems so counterintuitive, because retrocausality is so counterintuitive.”

There are two main conceptual ways to perceive retrocausality, says Adlam. One is the two-arrow case, “where you have two distinct and independent arrows of causation going in opposite directions,” she says. “The other scenario is a sort of all-at-once style of retrocausality, where we think of the laws of nature applying all together to the whole of history.”

While retrocausality is intertwined with the block universe idea, the latter could exist without the former being true. Adlam says the block universe interpretation focuses more on time than on the other laws of the universe. She compares the all-at-once idea of retrocausality to a game of Sudoku. “The rules of Sudoku apply to the whole grid, and just tell you whether a solution is valid or invalid,” she says. “The laws of nature could work like that. They could constrain all of spacetime.”

That does raise the question, though, of why we perceive a present—a here and now—in both the block universe and retrocausality concepts. Adlam says that might merely be a matter of perspective. In the same way that looking at the universe from another planet might look different, so too is looking at the universe from our given place in time.

“I am viewing the universe from a location here in California, and you are viewing the universe from some other location elsewhere in space,” she says. “Likewise, there’s a version of me viewing the universe from the 27th of October, and then tomorrow there’ll be another version of me viewing the universe from the 28th of October. They’re different perspectives on this big temporal block.”

It’s worth noting that the design of retrocausality does not allow for time travel. That’s because, if it were possible to send a signal into the past, it would result in a causal paradox where someone could prevent the action and its subsequent effect from taking place at all, says Adlam. That means that any possible retrocausation must be restricted to the hidden past, making it hard to measure.

Retrocausality and the block universe are understandably somewhat controversial ideas, and not everyone is convinced that they really exist. Not only do they bring up some potentially paradoxical scenarios but they also raise questions about whether the future is already fixed, and what that means for free will.

“One of the longstanding philosophical worries about the block universe is that everything is kind of fated and there’s no free will,” says Adlam. “In some sense it’s true,” she adds, but it “doesn’t necessarily mean that we don’t have free will. You have to think more carefully about what you would understand free will to be.”

For example, an idea called compatibilism says that we do have free will “as long as our desires and goals are the causes of our actions,” says Adlam. “It doesn’t matter if all of that was predetermined by physics. As long as I am the cause of my actions, I am acting freely.”

Perhaps future research will tell us more about whether we really live in a block universe, or if an effect can precede a cause. Or maybe that research has already been completed in another part of spacetime, in a cosmos where everything has happened—and is yet to happen, too.

Jonathan O'Callaghan is an award-winning freelance space journalist from the UK, currently based in Bangkok. His work has appeared in The New York Times, the BBC, Scientific American, New Scientist, and many other publications. He writes about astronomy, astrophysics, and space exploration, and sometimes appears on TV or radio as a space expert. He enjoys football, running, padel, spending time with his children, and exploring the world.

James Spader Has Never Been Afraid to Get Horny on Main

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The 66-year-old has spent four decades mastering the art of playing many different styles of pervert. VisionQuest proves he’s still got it.

IT CAN BE hard to keep track of the constantly shifting spaces of politics and culture, so you can be forgiven if you’ve missed arguably the biggest development in either space over the past few months, playing out as we speak: James Spader is back. The actor, who has spent four decades specializing in playing many different styles of pervert, has returned as yet another one in Disney+’s VisionQuest, the third and final chapter in a trilogy that also included WandaVision and Agatha All Along, and a work that might also be one of the first real considerations we’ve seen of a dystopian post-human world navigated exclusively by artificial intelligence agents. The show may not be for everyone, but Spader, as has often been the case since the ’80s, is doing truly unbelievable work.

Spader plays the physical manifestation of Ultron—the proto-AI doombot he voiced in 2015’s Avengers: The Age of Ultron, who has now assumed the form of James Spader, sporting a feral salt-and-pepper beard, hexagonal specs, and defense against the dark arts professor loungewear. He is glorious, bloviating on subjects from cyberpunk philosophy to fatherhood in a scotch and cigar baritone with a hint of mischief dancing behind his eyes.

With each passing VisionQuest episode, Spader not only helps push the show’s knotty plotting forward, but chews on each bit of scenery with the best of them (he’s particularly effective when sharing the screen with T'Nia Miller, who plays another high-minded scientist-turned-synthetic being). Spader’s history as the magnetic degenerate who captivated Hollywood in the ’80s and ’90s is more than evident in each episode of Marvel’s latest series, especially if you know where to look for it.

Spader, now 66, started his career as a calculating piece of shit in Pretty in Pink, before hitting it big on the American indie film scene, starting in Steven Soderbergh’s smash hit Sex, Lies & Videotape as, what else? An unnervingly direct, impotent drifter who interviews women on camera about their sexual experiences then beats off to the footage. From there he had brushes with conventional stardom in kind of blockbusters like Stargate, or, trying to belie his lot in life, miscast as straight men to Rob Lowe or John Cusack, but through the ’90s he becomes the maestro of the psychosexual thriller, showing up as obsessive compulsive rapist werewolves, hitchhiking gamblers, hooker-banging lawyers, and widowers who can’t get their face out of Susan Sarandon’s crotch. When you work on projects like these—writing about the entirety of a successful working actor’s filmography—there’s often a staggering level of diversity between genres and roles. What’s staggering about Spader’s career is how little of anything else there is. It’s all delinquent sicko shit.

It is poetic that for the most part, at the turn of the century, Spader migrates to television right around the time the sex thriller goes out of style. He ages, and wins long overdue acting trophies, on the small screen. Perhaps most interestingly, Spader now may be as interesting a presence as he ever has been. Through the years, he’s gained weight and lost hair and his voice has deepened, but he continues to ooze libertine, alpha sexuality and idiosyncratic intensity.

With his return to Marvel as a face, as well as a voice, the time was right to clarify, in the parlance of basketball clip packages on YouTube, “James Spader was a problem,” in several senses of the word. People today might not realize there was a time a Hollywood star could be defined by playing, and presenting to the public as a seductive, creepy, sex pest weirdo, and there was a steady diet of projects that gave them opportunities to do that specific thing on screen. In Spader’s work specifically, we see a moment in American life and culture when Hollywood still had the guts, and the temerity, to be horny on main.

Spader has referred to himself as “Brat Pack Adjacent,” emerging with that cohort of young stars but never quite “of” them. His primary moment comes in this John Hughes classic as an asshole who wears a blazer to school with the sleeves cuffed. He might be the gold standard of a quintessential sniveling ‘80s high school bully this side of William Zabka.

It makes sense that near the beginning, then at the outset of the late period of his career, Spader faced off against Robert Downey Jr., because in a certain light Spader can be seen as Downey’s slightly misshapen clone. Both are twitchy, motor-mouthed wise-asses incapable of a straight line delivery, who contain deep and limitless reserves of quippy charisma, only Spader’s juice is slightly tainted. Downey is right on the line of smarmy and seductive, and Spader is just over it. He’s alluring but a little off-putting, a little annoying, a little frightening, and always compelling. In Less Than Zero you worry for Downey, lost in Bret Easton Ellis’s debauched Los Angeles Neverland. You don’t worry about Spader, who is right where he wants to be, operating with impunity and ruling comfortably in hell.

Spader’s first mature role is less “Spadery” than basically anything he'd done before or would do afterward, thanks in large part to the way he weaponizes his soft-spoken openness. He’s the only character in the film who is honest about who he is and what he wants in a seedy drama where everyone lies or elides, talking around their desires.

Young Roland Emmerich was aiming for a dumbass version of an ‘80s Spielberg space adventure, so for his version of Richard Dreyfuss, he chose shaggy, adorable, baby James Spader in an era when oddballs like Nic Cage could become action stars. Spader went in a different direction with his career, but we’ll always have this performance as a linguist/allergy-prone dweeb with a shoulder-length shag presenting ancient aliens as an academic argument, using hieroglyphics to unlock an artifact liquid portal that transports you across the universe. He’s perfectly paired with Kurt Russell, as two different phenotypes of asshole.

No two movies have led to more hilarious mix-ups than this David Cronenberg-directed pervert classic, adapted from a J.G. Ballard novel about people with a desire for sex wrapped in a desire for self-immolation, and Paul Haggis’s shallow, sappy, Oscar-winning exploration of race relations of the exact same title. There’s too much to say about this film to confine it to a blurb, but I’ll just mention that a mere 25 minutes into the film, Spader is having sex with America’s Sweetheart, Holly Hunter. At this point, she is the widow of a man who died in a car accident with Spader. They are in a car identical to the one that killed her husband, one that Spader has just purchased and nearly wrecked again, with both Spader and Hunter in the car. One of the most depraved films I’ve ever seen. Watch it immediately.

Before moving to TV, Spader had a wild turn of the century: He’s in a lot of duds that don’t really know what to do with him. In a way, it exposes what a unique and specific tool he is, making his long, prolific career even harder to wrap your head around—because we see how easily it can go wrong for him. One is a bizarre ghost rom-com with Michael Caine and Maggie Smith, another is a near unwatchable post-Seven thriller with an egregiously bad Keanu Reeves as the antagonist killer, and the last, an abhorrent sex comedy. It’s interesting to watch Spader fail, trying to fight against his nature and remake himself into something closer to a conventional leading man. Luckily, he found a medium that better understood how to channel his dark peculiarities.

Another fetish movie, this one based on a Mary Gaitskill short story. Or, as I like to think of it, “Straight Pillion.” Spader is perfectly measured here as a stammering, mean, tender dom that uses his OCD in a film about control and submission.

After a test run on The Practice, this is the beginning of James Spader: TV star, the dawn of the persona that will become his final form. The show may appear to be a conventional primetime legal drama, and sometimes it is, but it possesses deranged and horny mid-aughts sexual politics that would cause riots if it came on air today. This is because it feels tailored to Spader (and co-star William Shatner) and his various kinks and proclivities.

“There is no such thing as a product, don’t ever think there is. There is only sex. Everything is sex. You understand that what I’m telling you is a universal truth?” This is how much of a generation of Americans first meets Spader, as the enigmatic boss daddy Robert California, on an NBC sitcom that was averaging nearly seven million viewers per episode at the time. When Steve Carell exited The Office, Ed Helms stepped into his role in Dunder Mifflin’s office as well as the sitcom’s dynamic as goofy office manager. But Spader also came along, injecting something entirely different. As California, he’s a literal confidence man whose tieless collar is unbuttoned, his receding hair is tousled, and he holds a dark hypnotic power over the office—one not even John Krasinski’s cool-headed Jim is immune to. Throughout the show’s eighth season, the entire office competes for the favor of Spader’s expert power-dynamic manipulator, a soft reprisal of his role in Secretary.

After spending weeks delving into this strange, delightful filmography, it pains me to think there are many people out there of a certain age who will mainly think of Spader as “The Blacklist guy,” but that’s what 218 episodes and 10 years in a role will do to your public perception. Despite frequent notices it was filming in my neighborhood for many years, I never caught this show on air. Having screened a few for consideration, I now see it as both a step down, and a fulfillment of Spader’s whole career journey. On one hand, he’s something of a diet Hannibal Lecter, a mastermind criminal who turns himself over to the Feds to help take down criminals worse than he is (for reasons that aren’t initially clear). Over time, the show exposes its conventional, maudlin heart through made-for-TV beats. Spader is fully washed at this late date in life, and should be commended for not fighting that. Still, he’s always dominant, remaining a few steps ahead of his friends and foes, a high T individual with a working cock, using it frequently.

In other words, he remains a glorious deviant—to the bitter end.

STAT+: Pharma critic Bernie Sanders is set to hold major sway over FDA funding bill

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Margaret is a Washington correspondent covering the Food and Drug Administration and other federal health agencies. Reach her on Signal at margaretmanto.61.

WASHINGTON – Democrats’ landslide victory in the midterm elections may have significant implications for the upcoming negotiations over a key funding source for the Food and Drug Administration, in part by empowering a major critic of the program.

The FDA collects user fees from pharmaceutical companies to fund the agency’s reviews of their medicines. The Prescription User Fee Drug Act must be renewed before the current law expires on Sept. 30. Otherwise, it could disrupt the FDA’s ability to approve medicines.

The power to shape that legislation will fall to senior Democrats, including Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.), who will likely lead the Senate’s health committee. Sanders is one of the few lawmakers who have opposed the core premise of the user fee structure.

Iraola Must Axe One Of His Liverpool Favourites, Only Van Dijk Has More Minutes

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Liverpool suffered a disappointing 3-2 defeat against Turkish side Fenerbahce in the Champions League on Wednesday night.

The Reds opened the scoring thanks to a fantastic Trey Nyoni volley, but they were pegged back just moments later as Mason Greenwood's deflected free kick found its way into the back of the net.

Liverpool then found themselves back in front as substitute Alexander Isak headed into the back of the net, but they once again conceded just a few minutes later.

English left-back Archie Brown scored an equaliser for Fenerbahce before Vedat Muriqi made it 3-2.

Manager Andoni Iraola will be incredibly disappointed that his side threw away a lead twice. There's no doubt there were some poor performances out there.

The result made it three games without a win in normal time for Liverpool after they drew with Chelsea (although they won on penalties) before suffering a defeat in the Premier League against champions Arsenal.

One player whose performances have been questioned of late is goalkeeper Alisson Becker. The Brazilian international simply hasn't been up to scratch in recent months, and it could be time for him to have a spell out of the team.

Alisson could undoubtedly have done better for multiple goals against Fenerbahce, while he's looked incredibly shaky overall.

Alisson also failed to adjust his feet and get back into position for Kai Havertz's goal for Arsenal at the weekend in the Premier League.

The 34-year-old has undoubtedly been one of the best goalkeepers of the Premier League era, but that doesn't mean he's invincible anymore.

Liverpool brought in substitute goalkeeper Giorgi Mamardashvili as a long-term replacement for Alisson, but it could be time to give him more of a regular run-out in the Premier League and Champions League.

Alisson simply hasn't been good enough, and although he's one of Iraola's favourites, the Liverpool manager might need to make a tough decision.

Only Virgil van Dijk has managed more minutes in all competitions than Alisson so far this season, with Mamardashvili only utilised in cup competitions.

Alisson has been the number one goalkeeper for both the Premier League and Champions League, but that shouldn't continue for much longer.

Liverpool are plotting a move to sign Real Madrid's Federico Valverde next summer with a £83million bid on the table

Nobody can take anything away from what Alisson has achieved in the game. The Brazilian still deserves plenty of respect for what he's done for the club, but there is always going to be a time when he naturally filters out of the side.

That might come sooner than Liverpool might have expected, but there's absolutely no doubt that he's dropped off this season.

Liverpool need to be preparing for the long term, and giving Mamardashvili more regular minutes could be hugely beneficial.

Whether Mamardashvili is capable of stepping up remains to be seen, but he's produced some strong performances for Liverpool since joining the club.

Google Tells Schools They Can Reject AI—But They'll Lose Search, Too

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Google Tells Schools They Can Reject AI—But They'll Lose Search, Too

Google is giving schools more freedom to reject artificial intelligence—provided they're also willing to give up Google Search, which is arguably what everybody in the world uses Google for.

The company announced Tuesday that its AI products will be disabled by default for K-12 student accounts unless administrators explicitly enable them. But schools that want to ban AI entirely will also have to disable Google's search engine, a tool millions of students rely on for everyday research.

Chris Phillips, Google's vice president and general manager of education, tried to justify the reasoning during a meeting with educators.

“Search and AI are deeply integrated,” he said according to Education Week. Schools uncomfortable with AI can choose not to enable Search at all.

Google says administrators will be able to activate or disable Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Search individually. That means schools can block the standalone chatbots while keeping Search available, but they cannot remove AI from Search itself.

For teachers who want students to research sources without receiving AI-generated answers, the company is effectively offering two options: accept its AI-powered search or find another search engine.

The announcement is already drawing accusations that Google is using its dominance in education to push a technology many schools are still trying to understand, regulate or restrict.

Google says the changes are intended to address concerns that AI chatbots can undermine learning by giving students ready-made answers instead of encouraging them to solve problems themselves.

Beginning in early 2027, schools that enable Search will be offered versions with conventional AI features, such as AI Overviews and AI Mode, or the education-focused Guided Learning experience, according to The New York Times.

Neither option provides the traditional search experience without generative AI.

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani accused the company of “trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools,” according to CBS News.

Former Federal Trade Commission chair Lina Khan was equally critical, accusing Google of using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI.

The criticism comes as some of America's largest school districts are actively restricting generative AI. New York City, for example, imposed a one-year moratorium on student-facing generative AI through eighth grade, covering nearly 600,000 students, while Los Angeles has also restricted the technology.

Los Angeles officials had specifically hoped to preserve Google Search while removing AI-generated summaries. Under the newly announced controls, that won't be an option.

Google's position also arrives amid continuing antitrust scrutiny. A federal court ruled in 2024 that Google unlawfully monopolized the U.S. search market, and the company has faced separate legal challenges over its decision to integrate AI summaries into search results.

School administrators are expected to receive the new controls in early 2027. They will be able to decide which AI products students can access, and even apply different settings by grade level. A Google Search option without AI, however, is not among the choices.

That said, for schools that want to, ahem, unofficially ban AI without losing Google search, our guide may provide a quick solution. It takes 5 minutes and lasts forever.

21 Thanksgiving Table Scripts For The Relative Who Always Starts Sh*t

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

“I didn’t know people I knew held these types of views” is a zinger.

It happens every year but it never gets less jarring. That one drunk uncle brings up politics (and never in a thoughtful way). Your mother-in-law asks about that one sensitive subject that’s really none of her business. Your grandma comments on how much food is on your teen daughter’s plate. Whatever the topic, family gatherings have a way of bringing a bunch of people with differing opinions around one table, where they’re just comfortable enough with everyone to spew their hot takes and outdated perspectives. If you have someone like this in your family, here are some Thanksgiving table scripts to respond to that one relative who always starts sh*t.

There’s nothing worse than when that one pot-stirring family member crosses you and you immediately feel your temper flare red hot. Or you get completely flustered and fumble for words. When someone pushes a boundary of yours, take a beat before responding.

“Pause, take a breath, and press your feet into the ground to feel a bit more grounded. Give yourself permission to slow down and stay in your body so you don't feel pressured to keep answering or discussing something you're not comfortable with. You don't owe anyone at the dinner table answers you aren't comfortable giving,” says Chloë Bean, LMFT, a somatic trauma therapist in Los Angeles.

You’re not alone in feeling like the holidays are a hotbed for family conflict, experts say.

“Perhaps things start off great, but after a few rounds of seasonal cocktails, Uncle So-and-So and Grandma Such-and-Such start spouting off some out-of-pocket sh*t. This has only increased as our socio-political climate intensifies and the gap between generational understandings widens,” says Whitney Polich, LICSW, licensed clinical social worker. “Despite this happening every year, perhaps we are still a bit gobsmacked and unsure of what to say, until the drive home when the perfect clapback comes to mind.”

Whether you clap back in the moment and escalate or would rather de-escalate, practicing a few responses in your head (or even in the mirror) ahead of the gathering can help you stay grounded and protect your peace.

There are three genres of response you could go with here, says Ashley Hamm, LPC, a licensed professional counselor who specializes in anxiety and people-pleasing.

“Sometimes acting surprised or baffled is enough to throw someone off-kilter and get them off the subject. This is especially useful if someone is sharing something problematic that they might know deep down is inappropriate to share,” says Hamm.

Try these: “I’m surprised you feel comfortable sharing ideas like that.” “I have no idea what you are talking about.” “I’m surprised to hear you say that; I didn’t know people I knew held these types of views.”

Keep a few alternate subjects in the back of your mind in case you need a quick diversion — a conversational smoke bomb, if you will.

Think: “Speaking of the news, I saw a really funny story about…” “Speaking of the news, I saw a really heartwarming story about…” “I’d rather not talk about our opinions on that because I don’t get to see you very often, and I’d rather hear about what you’ve been up to personally!”

“Simply acting like you didn’t hear them is a great strategy to stay disentangled from sensitive or divisive topics. Using some of the changing topics ideas above, you can simply start a new topic, or respond to the most recent unproblematic thing someone said,” Hamm says.

For example: “Oh, Aunt Mary, you were just talking about your recipe for the green bean casserole. I’d love to hear more about where you found it.” “Hmm, not sure what to say to that. Anyways, I’ve been trying to watch more movies lately. Has anyone seen anything good?”

Here are even more replies to consider from Polich and Bean:

Bookmark ‘em for your family’s Thanksgiving and thank yourself later.

Scientists find the best evidence yet of potentially habitable volcanic vents on Mars

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Magma and a possible environment for ancient life might be present on Mars in the region of a large volcano on the Red Planet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A noxious gas found seeping through cracks on Mars hints that there may be magma flowing beneath the Red Planet's surface — and perhaps even an environment similar to habitable ones on Earth, a new study suggests.

Europe's ExoMars Trace Gas Orbiter (TGO) spacecraft spotted a possible signal of hydrogen chloride gas leaking from rifts in the region of Alba Mons, the largest volcano on Mars in terms of surface area. (Mars' famous Olympus Mons is the largest volcano in terms of height.) Scientists interpret this gas as emissions from hot magma below the regolith of Mars.

Although more research is required to confirm the emissions, the team suggested that Alba Mons may host an environment similar to hydrothermal vents on Earth, where life once likely arose and continues to thrive today.

The study, published in the journal Science Advances Wednesday (Nov. 4), is not the first to suggest that magma may be flowing on Mars; NASA's InSight lander interpreted its presence through seismic observations and modeling earlier this year. But the new findings are the strongest evidence yet of gases being released by dormant volcanoes on Mars, first study author Alexander Trokhimovskiy, at Russia's Space Research Institute, told Live Science.

"Our study offers a complementary and potentially independent line of evidence [of Martian magma]: Atmospheric observations that reveal a possible signature of gas release from a subsurface magmatic system," Trokhimovskiy wrote in an email.

TGO first detected hydrogen chloride gas in Mars' atmosphere in 2021, but scientists were hesitant to link it to volcanic activity. The gas appeared to be seasonal, its distribution was not localized at a possible volcanic region, and other signs of volcanic gases, like sulfur, were not present, study co-author Kevin Olson, a visiting researcher at the University of Oxford who studies Mars gases, told Live Science in a separate email.

A photo of Mars with the Tharsis region centered.

Further work, however, suggested that the gas wasn't just popping up in the warmer spring and summer in Mars' southern hemisphere; it also appeared in the winter in the northern hemisphere, he noted.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

"I managed to catch two observations from the start of northern winter in the first Mars year of our mission, and a single observation the next year," Olson said, adding that a year on Mars is closer to two years on Earth due to the planet's wider orbit. "These out-of-season measurements were close to one another in location and time in both years, which was rather surprising. We then set about creating a campaign of special, targeted measurements at this time and place."

That campaign lasted two Mars years — roughly four Earth years — and increased the likelihood that scientists are seeing "an injection" of hydrogen chloride into the atmosphere from volcano-adjacent vents, Olson said.

The scientists confirmed the unseasonal observations in the northern hemisphere, finding a "distinct and pronounced signal" that then diminishes and falls below the spacecraft's detection threshold. "This inconsistency is more suggestive of a puff of gas entering the atmosphere than of regular atmospheric chemistry," he said. "While the link remains a hypothesis, we have now seen this gas appear locally, and nowhere else, over four Mars years of observations."

Daniel Mège, also a co-author of the paper and a professor who studies planetary geology at the Polish Academy of Sciences, identified the fissures where the gas may be emerging. He said the complementary evidence from InSight (on the ground) and TGO (circling above) helps give the study weight: InSight spotted activity in the crust, while TGO detected the emissions in the atmosphere.

Although magma activity is not in itself a sign of life, Mège noted that Earth's first life-forms came from sites like hydrothermal zones, which have a similar confluence of materials and energy as the potential vents on Mars. But conditions would need to be favorable in that area for at least millions of years to allow life to form, he cautioned.

"If these requirements are met at local scale, at the scale of the seepage structures, then living organisms might wait for us to discover them," he told Live Science in an email. "But maintaining stable conditions over such a long period at a very local scale is not obvious."

US midterm tsunami sweeps away video game composer who talked about ‘double booty’ and made sure to never touch anyone, just loom’

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Marty O’Donnell’s bid for Nevada’s 3rd Congressional District ended in disappointment—for Marty—as he’s one of many Republican candidates who got swept away by a massive blue wave that kept Democratic incumbent Susie Lee’s seat after a 20-percent margin of victory.

You may not recognize Marty’s name, but chances are you’re familiar with his work. He served as Bungie’s main composer for over two decades, responsible for Destiny‘s soundtrack and, more importantly, Halo’s hauntingly beautiful main theme. Unfortunately, this really is a prime candidate for separating the art from the artist.

The first possibility is that Marty had received a glowing endorsement from Donald Trump shortly before the election.

It is my Great Honor to endorse America First Patriot, Marty O'Donnell, who is running to represent the incredible people of Nevada’s 3rd Congressional District. Marty is running against Susie Lee, a true Radical Left Extremist who does not represent the Values of Southern…

Unfortunately for O’Donnell, he was gunning for Nevada, a district that was greatly impacted by Trump’s unwavering stance on tariffs, so maybe that’s not the guy you want to seem very well connected to at that point. Still, there are other equally important reasons to point out, like the fact that O’Donnell is reportedly not someone you want close, or even “looming” around, especially if you’re a woman.

A few of his ex-colleagues have talked about prior experiences, none of them good. The scariest part, however, is that Marty might already be so familiar with these situations that he’s already devised a game plan.

When talking about a particularly infamous event at a Bungie Halloween party, Marty said, “Yes, the women got really creeped out but I made sure to never touch anyone, just loom.”

Oh, and that’s all after he’d become a pioneer in appropriating Halo imagery to bastardize it with AI to create anti-immigration propaganda.

After her tremendous victory, Susie Lee likely destroyed any horcruxes Marty likely still had lying around by tagging him in a tweet showing a viral video where a bunch of school kids perform a surprisingly beautiful rendition of the Halo theme inside a bathroom. The video fades to black with the message “the fight is finished.”

Enjoy your retirement @MartyTheElder pic.twitter.com/vH8KwKWrdl

I usually prefer when winners celebrate with grace and avoid teabagging their enemies, but Susie and so many others have been through so much crap because of “MartyTheElder” that I just cannot condemn anyone for double-tapping the guy.

As for Marty himself, well, he’d previously stated that he never really wanted to be a politician in the first place, so, if we’re to take his words for it, then everyone’s all the better for this result. Yes, even the people who cannot stop talking about how you shouldn’t be mixing politics with video games.

Tiago is a freelancer who used to write about video games, cults, and video game cults. He now writes for Destructoid in an attempt to find himself on the winning side when the robot uprising comes.
